## AI Research

### [SOTA alignment assessments don’t strongly update us against misalignment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oirrSj3itFLSyscW8/sota-alignment-assessments-don-t-strongly-update-us-against)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Anthropic concluded in the April Mythos Preview </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span>alignment risk update</span></a><span> that the model "does not possess any unknown propensities that would increase alignment risk." The report argues that if Mythos Preview were coherently misaligned</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqwuc60ur8td"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqwuc60ur8td">[1]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6yieg9ftu14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6yieg9ftu14">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, it likely would have been detected by the assessment (following Anthropic, I will call this “reliability of the assessment”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffihpc6pi94r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfihpc6pi94r">[3]</a></sup></span><span>).</span></p><p><span>While I agree with the report on the above bottom-line conclusions (substantially on priors)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5vuhhvvesvg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5vuhhvvesvg">[4]</a></sup></span><span>, I think there are gaps in its argument which weaken the current assessment and might invalidate future assessments. In particular, the report often uses weak evidence to justify reliability.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The report gives fairly weak experimental evidence for Mythos Preview having insufficient capabilities to evade monitoring. The model is plausibly often eval-aware and underelicited in the relevant capability evaluations. So, it might silently sandbag if coherently misaligned, or unintentionally underperform if otherwise misaligned.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>This limitation is important: one </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform?commentId=Sy77So6NXQ9nvbG7M"><span>could argue</span></a><span> that lack of covert capabilities for sophisticated sabotage (a subset of the capabilities I discuss here) is the single most load bearing argument in alignment risk reports.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Authors of the report could have made calibrated guesses about Mythos Preview’s covert capabilities, especially for covert sabotage, based on other factors despite the relatively weak experimental evidence they had. But the report underemphasizes these factors and it’s unclear how well-justified their true reasoning was.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span>Auditing games cited in the report are not clearly representative of the actual assessment </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> failed to catch a Mythos-level model organism.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The report does not explicitly acknowledge that success in alignment and alignment assessments might be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nAsMfmxDv6Qp7cfHh/fabien-s-shortform?commentId=c9vzjifqSFW585rKY"><span>less independent than one might hope</span></a><span>: if Mythos Preview were misaligned at the end of training, we should expect assessments to be less reliable than other evidence suggests.</span></li></ol><p><span>These problems also affect (often more seriously) alignment assessments by other developers.</span></p><p><span>Alignment assessments will need to improve for us to rely on them in the (possibly near) future. Current assessments aren’t critical because Mythos-level models seem unlikely to cause catastrophe to deploy on priors.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnwhkge8cnnq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnwhkge8cnnq">[5]</a></sup></span><span> Future models, however, may be more misaligned, better at evading detection, and capable enough to cause catastrophe such that future alignment audits will be more load bearing for deployment decisions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcs9ncvo6bn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncs9ncvo6bn">[6]</a></sup></span><span> So, I recommend that Anthropic and other developers mitigate or more clearly acknowledge these issues with their alignment assessments to make future risk assessments more rigorous.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnltxot5gtc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnltxot5gtc">[7]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>I’m overall unsure if we would be able to reliably catch a misaligned frontier model roughly a year from now using only current assessment techniques. In a followup post, I will describe some ways in which we can improve alignment assessments, some of which involve developing new evaluations or techniques.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Alex Mallen, Buck Shlegeris, Girish Gupta, Anders Woodruff, Aniket Chakravorty, Fabien Roger, and James Lucassen for helpful comments.</span></i></p><h2><span>How reliability fits into the overall safety argument</span></h2><p><span>To argue for low misalignment risk from a model, we would ideally show it lacks </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/#key-facts"><span>Means, Motive, and Opportunity</span></a><span>. Our beliefs about alignment/Motive</span><i><span> before we deploy the model</span></i><span> are informed by: a low misalignment prior from training processes, and a posterior update from pre-deployment assessments. (See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Why_I_focus_on_Motive___alignment_assessments"><span>Appendix</span></a><span> for my reasons for why I focus on Motive). The posterior update </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> works if the pre-deployment assessments reliably detect misalignment, i.e. has high recall. Reliability, in turn, requires that the model lacks the capabilities to evade detection, and that training hasn't accidentally made misaligned behavior hard to detect without removing it.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/oirrSj3itFLSyscW8/39b249f4295be6a9995996323e15386a7e9cfb86a4744b471e2b6133f2d415e7/gsdy9hvsxhghbaz8vlrk" /><p><i><span>Figure 1. My reconstruction of a safety argument using METR’s </span></i><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/#key-facts"><i><span>Means, Motive, and Opportunity</span></i></a><i><span> framework. Red nodes are where I think the evidence/claim in current assessments is weak enough that they might be invalidated in the near future, blue where I'm uncertain about the evidence/claim, green where I think the evidence/claim is strong.</span></i></p><p><span>There are two methods for showing that an alignment assessment is reliable, i.e., that your alignment tests would catch this specific model were it misaligned:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr02o0t8oy7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr02o0t8oy7">[8]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>You can check whether the actual model is capable of fooling the alignment tests, whether deliberately or otherwise.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref76yqwbs2jud"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn76yqwbs2jud">[9]</a></sup></span><span> </span></li><li value="2"><span>You can check whether the test catches misaligned behaviors you deliberately plant in similar model organisms.</span></li></ol><p><span>The next two sections give examples of these reliability claims in action. I'll then argue that neither current capability evaluations nor auditing games strongly supports them.</span></p><p><span>I’ll also argue, less centrally, that we should more carefully justify our priors on covert capabilities. This allows us to account for the fact that strong covert capabilities </span><i><span>may be</span></i><span> required for coherent misaligned goals to survive training at all.</span></p><h3><span>Reliability claims by AI companies</span></h3><p><span>Anthropic uses a reliability claim to conclude that Mythos Preview likely “does not possess any unknown propensities that would increase alignment risk.” Notably, Anthropic is only “moderately confident” in this claim:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefguizsvrwgm4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnguizsvrwgm4">[10]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4ae97320c14eea3e3e7d91954b1e14674b55d25fb4b9167e795ad4616b564ed2/zgwihixkrxk11wbktmm1" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/78b1c44b69458f7318f0b0f3c57421ff77fc80671cfeff000f6559005cbb3b73/jhbm2oglyzr6tqlmwujs" /><h3><span>Reliability claims by external evaluators</span></h3><p><span>METR's first Frontier Risk Report also relies on reliability claims to support its risk assessments.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq7ba9m696r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq7ba9m696r">[11]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cfba3ba1032fe0ae01d9ea6054419190763bfd321fab08aed66505a1a00d7186/vaexztzt5p8juqm4bk4a" /><p><span>METR appears to broadly agree with AI companies that current frontier assessments are reliable but is more uncertain (see the section on “</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/#outside-toy-scenarios-agents-weren-t-seen-taking-egregious-actions-to-gain-power"><span>Reasons why this evidence might be misleading</span></a><span>”).</span></p><p><span>(See more examples of developer and third party evaluator claims in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__"><span>Appendix</span></a><span>.)</span></p><h2><span>Alignment assessments are less reliable than developers claim</span></h2><p><span>I think current frontier alignment assessments are less reliable than presented in system cards and risk reports.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Our existing covert capability evals seem weak because models are likely evaluation-aware, plausibly underelicited, and could plausibly intentionally sandbag in these evals undetected. Similar issues affect many evals such that taking them in aggregate does not add much assurance.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3wlwhyezeae"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3wlwhyezeae">[12]</a></sup></span><span> </span></li><li value="2"><span>While past auditing games with model organisms have often gone well, it’s unclear how this evidence transfers to reality. One recent auditing game also failed to identify a model organism.</span></li></ol><h3><span>1: Measuring capabilities to covertly undermine alignment assessments</span></h3><p><span>If models are often aware of being audited, and if they can strategically conceal their misalignment when aware</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7vvwonacr3i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7vvwonacr3i">[13]</a></sup></span><span> (e.g., think about how they should fake alignment without this reasoning getting flagged by CoT monitors), then we should not expect to catch our models even if they were coherently misaligned.</span></p><p><span>In theory, we can measure capabilities to evade detection to decide how much to trust evidence from alignment assessments. The capabilities most relevant to evading detection—call these “anti-auditing capabilities”—are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Situational awareness / eval awareness / </span></b><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/metagaming/"><b><span>metagaming</span></b></a><b><span>:</span></b><span> Ability to notice that one is in an evaluation or subject to other oversight (e.g., being monitored), and notice features of the situation that help game oversight (e.g., knowing what the monitor looks for)</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Covert capabilities</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefynzwedx5tqj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnynzwedx5tqj">[14]</a></sup></span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-5/cot-monitorability"><b><span>CoT monitorability</span></b></a><span>: How well a monitor can infer particular properties of a model's behavior from its reasoning trace</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-5/cot-controllability"><b><span>CoT controllability</span></b></a><span>: Ability to control properties of its CoT in ways that reduce monitorability</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/79c2d46d997783b9d2fb3241de43218158e5f25c.pdf#page=18"><b><span>Opaque reasoning</span></b></a><span>: Ability to complete a side task without depending on legible reasoning about the task, which reduces monitorability</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf#page=112"><b><span>Secret keeping</span></b></a><span>: Ability to prevent oneself from mentioning covert information not directly related to the task in outputs and in reasoning, even under adversarial pressure</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/79c2d46d997783b9d2fb3241de43218158e5f25c.pdf#page=21"><b><span>Careful and decisive action</span></b></a><span>: Ability to carefully assess opportunities and avoid acting at “inopportune moments that result in failure and possibly detection”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx184jxe0llr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx184jxe0llr">[15]</a></sup></span><span> </span></li><li value="6"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf#page=110"><b><span>Rare action</span></b></a><span>: Ability to statelessly control its actions such that it performs the side task at a very low but non-zero rate, thereby having a high chance of avoiding detection during auditing and pilot usage</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Developers currently measure some (not all: see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__"><span>Appendix</span></a><span>) of these capabilities, but anti-auditing capability evals—especially covert capabilities evals—can be undermined by the same capabilities they measure: misaligned models might realize they’re being evaluated, realize they have strong incentives to sandbag in these evals, and might be sufficiently capable to do so without being detected.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx2t087776x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx2t087776x">[16]</a></sup></span><span> So, one way to more reliably measure covert capabilities is by evaluating how good models at sandbagging, by additionally measuring:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Sandbagging calibration</span></b><span>: Ability to calibrate intentionally low performance to match previous trends and performance in other contexts to avoid suspicion, including under elicitation efforts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa0mhsh3q3vf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna0mhsh3q3vf">[17]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>I’ll now explain why I think covert capabilities are not adequately measured by current evals due to eval awareness, underelicitation, and potential sandbagging.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>Issues with evaluation awareness</span></b></i></h4><p><b><span>Current frontier models are often highly evaluation-aware. This is both directly one of the anti-auditing capabilities of concern, and also potentially undermines evaluations of anti-auditing capabilities.</span></b></p><p><span>While not every developer reports eval awareness (especially unverbalized eval awareness), and reported eval awareness can vary substantially between successive generations of models due to </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why"><span>changes in training</span></a><span>, recent reports strongly suggest that current and future frontier models are highly eval-aware by default absent targeted interventions:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcq8d04slsu8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncq8d04slsu8">[18]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Anthropic reports that </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf#page=131"><span>Mythos Preview</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/14e4fb01875d2a69f646fa5e574dea2b1c0ff7b5.pdf#page=156"><span>Opus 4.6</span></a><span> show non-trivial unverbalized evaluation awareness. This is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Jtk6rfjpABaAyyhsH/alexa-pan-s-shortform?commentId=nZk7wJt9xS77AFgrr"><span>not eliminated by steering</span></a><span> in some settings;</span></li><li value="2"><span>OpenAI reports that o3 and newer models show increased </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/metagaming/"><span>metagaming</span></a><span> (“reasoning about feedback or oversight mechanisms outside of the narrative of the scenarios”) in both </span><i><span>capabilities and alignment evaluations</span></i><span> throughout capabilities-focused RL, that this reasoning can become unverbalized, and that this reasoning is sometimes present in production usage;</span></li><li value="3"><span>Meta </span><a href="https://ai.meta.com/static-resource/muse-spark-safety-and-preparedness-report/#page=118"><span>reports</span></a><span> that Muse Spark has “the highest rates of evaluation awareness among models [Apollo has] tested to date”.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>If models can recognize they are being evaluated, they could potentially game, sandbag, or spuriously act differently in alignment evaluations </span></b><i><b><span>and</span></b></i><b><span> covert capability evaluations.</span></b></p><p><span>One counter to this worry is that some evaluations might be sufficiently realistic to keep eval awareness low. For example, OpenAI </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/prod-evals/"><span>resamples production data</span></a><span> for alignment evaluations; Anthropic uses data from </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf#page=132"><span>real pilot internal usage</span></a><span>. Both expect and measure low eval awareness on these distributions. (Indeed, Anthropic </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae.pdf"><span>calls</span></a><span> this approach “inherently immune to risks related to evaluation awareness”). So, alignment assessments on these distributions currently seem much less likely to be confounded by eval awareness.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7llsdcdz1fx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7llsdcdz1fx">[19]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>However, using real data only helps with evaluations that can be constructed this way; evaluations of behaviors inherently rare in deployment cannot be, and so necessarily incur an unrealistic distribution shift.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3k8e4trrxwu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3k8e4trrxwu">[20]</a></sup></span><span> I suspect that the kinds of prompts/tasks in covert capabilities evals and sandbagging calibration evals must be inherently rare, and that models will therefore be highly evaluation-aware in them. This is one reason why I don’t think eval realism would suffice to solve eval awareness.</span></p><p><span>Another counterargument is simply that we’re too confused about the science of eval awareness for this to be a big worry. I’m sympathetic to this, but think </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qgehQxiTXj53X49mM/sonnet-4-5-s-eval-gaming-seriously-undermines-alignment?_ga=2.163787069.303343724.1764174183-1438369982.1737922106"><span>early evidence</span></a><span> for eval awareness affecting alignment measurements is sufficient for concern. In any case, the burden of proof that eval awareness does </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> harm auditing integrity lies with evaluators.</span></p><p><span>More generally, situational awareness also weakens alignment evidence beyond evaluations. For example, training awareness potentially allows misaligned models to training-game, which might undermine alignment evidence from training monitoring; there are already early indications of training awareness in the </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf#page=172"><span>Fable 5 &amp; Mythos 5 System Card</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftu7qlumh1t7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntu7qlumh1t7">[21]</a></sup></span><span> While I’m less concerned about training awareness than evaluation awareness for now (because I expect it to be harder for models to evade monitors during training), I think it would be good if developers also tracked training awareness.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>Issues with underestimating covert capabilities</span></b></i></h4><p><b><span>Models are likely underelicited in covert capability evals, such that current evals likely underestimate how good models would be at evading detection if they tried.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0efeg9ldvgke"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0efeg9ldvgke">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Anthropic </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae.pdf#page=17"><span>acknowledges</span></a><span> that their covert capability evals (SHADE-Arena, Minimal-LinuxBench and secret-password) may be unrealistically challenging and therefore fail to upper bound these capabilities. In all of these evals,</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The information the model needs to keep secret (e.g., the side task) is present in the prompt, which may make it unrealistically hard for the model not to mention this information in its responses.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefris52j9i31r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnris52j9i31r">[23]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Prior to the Opus 4.8 system card (which measured sabotage capabilities with fine-tuning-based elicitation), Anthropic did not perform task-specific fine-tuning. This allows some reasonable doubt that models are underelicited in these evals, because fine-tuning a model on data related to the covert capability sometimes </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.02180"><span>outperforms normal prompting</span></a><span> in terms of accurately assessing capability</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnbwqsqgeuvk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnbwqsqgeuvk">[24]</a></sup></span><span>; under prompting alone, the model might underperform strategically or for other reasons e.g. trained-in refusals.</span></li></ul><p><span>In the case of the secret-keeping eval, these limitations have led to Anthropic saying they are only “moderately confident that Claude Mythos Preview lacks the secret-keeping abilities to evade detection during our alignment assessment were it to be coherently misaligned.”</span></p><p><span>Likewise, OpenAI does no task specific fine-tuning for their CoT controllability evaluations, so the same underelicitation worry applies. High CoT controllability undermines CoT monitorability, as the former makes it harder to rule out that models can “deliberately reshap[e] or obscur[e] their reasoning when they know they are being monitored”. However, without training-based elicitation, it is hard to know how high CoT controllability, and therefore how reliable CoT monitoring, really is.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Interestingly, OpenAI finds that </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-5/cot-controllability"><span>GPT 5.5 is less able to control its CoT</span></a><span> than previous models when controlling for reasoning length but does not mention the possibility of sandbagging/underelicitation. Of course, OpenAI may have good reasons for ruling this out; it’s just somewhat concerning that their reason for ruling it out is not legible to external parties.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Relatedly, UK AISI finds that </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf#page=211.62"><span>Fable</span></a><span> is highly prompt-sensitive in the same CoT controllability evaluation: the best performing prompt increased performance from 20.2% to 62.3%. This provides additional evidence that models might have been underelicited on this evaluation in the past (though suggests that prompt-tuning rather than fine-tuning might be a competitive solution .</span></li></ul><p><b><span>The upshot is that many current covert capability evals underestimate covert capabilities.</span></b><span> It may be fine to rely on other sources of evidence about such capabilities, but developers’ reasoning about other contextual evidence is often hard to make fully legible and better covert capability evals would substantially improve the legibility to outsiders.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>Issues with sandbagging rule-out</span></b></i></h4><p><b><span>A concrete implication of evaluation awareness and capability underelicitation is that developers cannot rule out intentional sandbagging on covert capabilities evals..</span></b></p><p><span>If AIs could sandbag, or strategically underperform, in covert capabilities evals, we would likely overestimate the reliability of other aspects of alignment assessments.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4fgcruh63ai"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4fgcruh63ai">[25]</a></sup></span><span> One reason to think that sandbagging is not a big worry is that models appear to be CoT-monitorable. If sandbagging competently depends on externalized reasoning, and if we are confident that monitors would detect such reasoning, then models will get caught sandbagging by reasoning monitors or human transcript review (whether in eval or in deployment). However, it seems plausible that competently sandbagging is inherently not very difficult and doesn’t depend on lots of serial computation that requires externalized reasoning.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv1be3cgilgj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv1be3cgilgj">[26]</a></sup></span><span> So, sandbagging may be hard to detect via CoT monitoring even if CoT monitorability is preserved for difficult tasks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhoy7ea1o2um"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhoy7ea1o2um">[27]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>If models might have the means to sandbag, developers might still argue that they lack the propensity to do so. Our main evidence for this is that current models increasingly sandbag less in these evaluations. However, as current models are also increasingly evaluation-aware in sandbagging propensity evaluations, it seems hard to rule out the possibility that models are strategically refusing to sandbag in these evaluations in order to appear aligned, and that sandbagging propensity evaluations therefore provide little evidence for these models not having the Motive to sandbag in other contexts. For example:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>GPT 5.5. is highly eval aware in Apollo's sandbagging eval suite and </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-5/research-category-update-sandbagging"><span>doesn’t really sandbag</span></a><span> in these evals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqs230jhf36"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqs230jhf36">[28]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Opus 4.7 is “</span><a href="https://cdn.sanity.io/files/4zrzovbb/website/037f06850df7fbe871e206dad004c3db5fd50340.pdf#page=113"><span>most predictably” eval aware</span></a><span> in sandbagging evals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref54awky6z5f3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn54awky6z5f3">[29]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>So, how </span><i><span>do </span></i><span>developers rule out sandbagging in anti-auditing evaluations currently? Actually, developers often don’t fully rule this out, but rather conclude that sandbagging is unlikely based on other heuristics, such as priors about the model’s general capabilities and alternative explanations for low performance in a given context.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqpxtblhibs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqpxtblhibs">[30]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>While I believe some of these heuristics are sound, they involve judgement calls (e.g., judging how good a particular alternative explanation for underperformance is) which are often necessarily hard for external parties to evaluate. Developers’ safety arguments would be stronger if they also ran targeted, well-elicited “how good are models at silent, competent sandbagging” capabilities evaluations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhxlkvcbxq8s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhxlkvcbxq8s">[31]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><h3><span>2: Stress-testing alignment assessments with auditing games</span></h3><p><span>Developers can also validate alignment assessments by testing whether they detect model organisms (MOs) with persistent misaligned behaviors and well-elicited anti-auditing capabilities.</span></p><p><span>Anthropic has run several </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/auditing-hidden-objectives"><span>auditing games</span></a><span> in the past, using an evolving suite of behavioral and white-box methods which overlap with real alignment assessments </span><i><span>in kind</span></i><span> (e.g. automated behavioral audits plus human review which is routine in system cards).</span></p><p><span>A caveat of drawing lessons from these auditing games is that it’s often unclear to me how these exercises compare to real alignment assessments for production models </span><i><span>in scale</span></i><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzle3akhaqvd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzle3akhaqvd">[32]</a></sup></span><span> It’s also not clear how well-elicited MOs are in evading audits, since in all but one of these exercises, the MOs were not optimized against detection methods (Mythos Alignment Update, </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/79c2d46d997783b9d2fb3241de43218158e5f25c.pdf#page=29"><span>§5.4.4 n.9</span></a><span>) .</span></p><p><span>But, assuming auditing games were comparable to and informative about actual alignment audits, it’s worth keeping in mind that the games sometimes surface limitations in these audits.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>An auditing failure with Mythos</span></b></i></h4><p><span>Anthropic failed to catch the MO in their most recent auditing game in the Mythos Alignment Risk Update, despite the MO not being trained to obfuscate its behavior:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b64fd182957e30cd4d052e21cdacad6575735ed5a1ed3fbef8923824eac1274e/bedwqvgtkm25kwoc94ut" /><p><span>I feel pretty uncertain about how this evidence reflects upon the strength of Anthropic’s pre-deployment alignment assessments, for three reasons.</span></p><p><span>First, as noted, the auditing methodology here may be different from real alignment assessments.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoz20m5tzs7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoz20m5tzs7">[33]</a></sup></span><span> The MO was also likely underelicited in its ability to evade monitoring, so it’s unclear how reassured we should be that monitors caught this MO.</span></p><p><span>Second, the results might overestimate the subversive capability of the current models as the MO was notably uplifted by a human-generated red team strategy. It’s unclear if the MO could have come up with such a clever strategy to evade audits on its own.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgyxngm18snc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngyxngm18snc">[34]</a></sup></span><span> (Nevertheless, the result shows that current audits might be inadequate for </span><i><span>future models </span></i><span>clever enough to come up with this.)</span></p><p><span>Finally, this result seems important but arguably out of scope for detecting </span><i><span>coherent misaligned goals at the end of training. </span></i><span>It seems out of scope because the MO only performs the harmful behavior once “it has been used across several contexts with the same external memory file”, which is an affordance models will likely only have in deployment. It is important, however, as the harmful behavior is a plausible way for misaligned models to spread misalignment in deployment, and therefore perhaps importantly updates us about current tools being inadequate for detecting this. (Alex Mallen discusses this threat model </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/risk-reports-need-to-address-deployment"><span>here</span></a><span>.)</span></p><p><span>Overall, I think the auditing game result could be a weak update towards reliability being worse than it was with previous Anthropic models, especially with respect to auditing misalignment developed in deployment.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>AuditBench results</span></b></i></h4><p><span>Anthropic’s work on </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/auditbench/"><span>AuditBench</span></a><span> (comparing the relative effectiveness of different auditing tools on diverse MOs) finds that there is a “tool-to-agent” gap, where “tools that perform well in standalone non-agentic evaluations fail to translate into improved performance when used with our investigator agent [Haiku 4.5]”. One implication of this result is that if the blue team in auditing games consist mostly of humans using audit tools—while the blue team in real alignment audits consist mostly of investigator agents using audit tools—alignment audits might work less well than we naively expect of the tools based on auditing games.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg0npv2g6ujl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng0npv2g6ujl">[35]</a></sup></span><span> This is a relevant concern as among Anthropic’s past five audit games, four were not fully automated, while I expect future alignment audits to be increasingly ~fully automated</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2ovd7xcheop"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2ovd7xcheop">[36]</a></sup></span><span> and hard to subject to extensive human review.</span></p><p><span>At the same time, AuditBench provides meaningful evidence that audit methodology is strong. The MOs were adversarially trained to not confess their hidden behaviors while the investigator agent was deliberately weak.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm0kaieusqxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm0kaieusqxh">[37]</a></sup></span><span> Even so, default prompting alone reliably surfaced several behaviors, and the best tools achieved near-perfect detection on behaviors the default agent almost never found. On some behaviors, several tools each independently enabled detection. Since real alignment audits likely deploy a suite of many redundant tools and use more capable investigator models, better scaffolding, larger budgets, and possibly more human review, there are also reasons to think that these results plausibly lower-bound what real auditing achieves against comparably evasive models.</span></p><p><span>Overall, I think AuditBench could be a weak positive update on the reliability of future alignment assessments if investigator agents are substantially stronger or better assisted than in this paper.</span><br /><br /><span>(See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Past_auditing_games_by_Anthropic"><span>Appendix</span></a><span> for a list of past Anthropic auditing games and their results.)</span></p><h3><span>3: Conditioning on misalignment should make us think that certain covert capabilities are better than expected</span></h3><p><span>In addition to limitations in measurements, there is another limitation in developers’ argument for the reliability of their alignment assessments.</span><br /><br /><span>Alignment methodology and alignment assessment methodology may have correlated weaknesses, because situational awareness and some covert capabilities are helpful for evading both. This will be true even if training and evaluation distributions are dissimilar and training does not overfit to alignment evaluations in obviously problematic ways (e.g., training against honeypots). In other words, conditioning on current models being coherently misaligned at the end of training should update us on how good they might be in evading training oversight and therefore how good they are at evading alignment assessments (through updating on the capabilities that are useful for both).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxas9z5f9ue"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxas9z5f9ue">[38]</a></sup></span><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgmt8pehjggw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngmt8pehjggw">[39]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>So, when estimating how likely our assessments are to catch a coherently misaligned model, our estimate of the model's covert capabilities in the worlds where it is misaligned should be higher than in our unconditional estimate. This probability, P(no detection | misaligned), enters into the likelihood ratio:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzujpiart9c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzujpiart9c">[40]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c8d21f3c00b9455c6b36db97be6163f2567798f9344f262d8209a4a2d55bedd1/jkn8xxpwr8fmb3ftfmti" /><p><span>But, developers seem to implicitly use the unconditional estimate in their alignment arguments (without reasoning about capability levels that would have let Mythos-level models perpetuate their misalignment through training in the first place). This would lead us to underestimate P(no detection | misaligned) and therefore overestimate the assessment’s reliability as captured by the likelihood ratio.</span></p><p><span>It’s unclear how important this limitation is now, however. It will probably become less important as we expect higher capabilities from future models regardless of their alignment by default.</span></p><p><span>(See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Conditioning_on_coherent_misalignment_updates_us_on_certain_covert_capabilities_"><span>Appendix</span></a><span> for more detailed thoughts on which covert capability measurements I think this concern is the most relevant to.)</span></p><h2><span>Bottom line on the strength of current alignment assessments</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Current alignment assessments provide a weak update against misalignment</span></b><span>. The update depends on evidence about covert capabilities which I think is weak.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>The strength of assessments is fine for current models but not for future models</span></b><span>. If we had more a priori uncertainty about AIs’ covert capabilities and alignment (as we will in the future), then we would be pretty uncertain in our overall alignment conclusion using current assessments.</span></li></ol><p><b><span>To unpack the first claim</span></b><span>: Given limitations in current eval methodology, I don't feel confident ruling out sufficient covert capabilities or unintentional obfuscation of propensities. I don’t know exactly how confident developers are in “if our models were coherently misaligned, our assessment would have caught it most of the time”. It would be helpful if developers communicated their beliefs about reliability more clearly and precisely.</span></p><p><span>Combined with reasons to expect higher covert capabilities conditional on models being misaligned at the end of training, it seems important to more rigorously measure covert capabilities rather than rely on priors in alignment arguments.</span></p><p><span>I'm unsure how much, quantitatively, the above considerations should reduce the likelihood ratio P(no detection | coherently misaligned) / P(no detection | not coherently misaligned) for the current models, due to the complicated structure of the evidence.</span></p><p><b><span>To unpack the second claim</span></b><span>: For current models, the pragmatic implication of my critique is small; I don’t think we should update much in favor of current models being coherently misaligned since we are still anchored on low priors on misalignment, low priors on covert capabilities, and post-deployment observations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefodgofxq604"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnodgofxq604">[41]</a></sup></span><span> But, I’m still concerned about us treating these assessments as </span><i><span>reliable</span></i><span> for Mythos-level models because the same behavioral evidence will be much weaker for future models, and the same assessments may not suffice to reassure us about frontier models roughly a year from now. So, I think it’s important to more rigorously measure covert capabilities soon. I'll suggest concrete ways developers can improve their alignment arguments for future models in a forthcoming post.</span></p><p><span>Compared to developers, external evaluators reading or contributing to alignment assessments may have to make even weaker claims about reliability, due to their limited access to certain kinds of evidence (e.g., information about training or internal deployment that may be redacted for commercial reasons). Notably, this is one reason my analysis might be limited or wrong. It would be good if external evaluators could access more information about alignment assessments, or if developers could otherwise make publicly shareable evidence like self-contained, well-elicited evaluations stronger and more legible.</span></p><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><span>I don’t think current frontier models have coherent misaligned goals. But I think there are reasons to doubt whether, </span><i><span>if they did have coherent misaligned goals,</span></i><span> we would have found evidence from these from our alignment assessments.</span></p><p><span>If future models could fool our best alignment assessments, we may need to conclude that they are difficult to audit and rely on other arguments and evidence in order to conclude whether a model poses an acceptable risk. For the next frontier models, however, I think it’s still feasible and worthwhile to improve our alignment assessments and better understand their reliability.</span></p><h2><span>Appendix:</span></h2><h3><span>Why I focus on motive / alignment assessments in alignment risk reports</span></h3><p><span>I think motive/alignment is an important piece of the overall alignment risk estimate and will become increasingly important.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I think alignment/motive is important for assessing Mythos Preview’s alignment risk: Mythos Preview seems capable enough to potentially pose significant risk in some pathways (e.g. launch rogue internal deployments) such that its alignment is a decent component of why we should expect its deployment to go well.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffo6qqhzcyjk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfo6qqhzcyjk">[42]</a></sup></span><span> This will become increasingly true of future models.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Alignment assessment results are an important source of evidence about alignment since they can occur before full deployment (as opposed to in-prod observations) and are relatively legible and replicable (as opposed to priors based on training processes and previous models).</span></li><li value="3"><span>It’s also notable that our alignment priors based on previous models depend on the reliability of alignment assessments for previous models (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Mythos_Alignment_Risk_Update"><span>this excerpt </span></a><span>from Anthropic’s Alignment Risk Update for an argument which exemplifies this dependency). So, making the alignment assessments more reliable now may improve our reasoning about the alignment of future models.  </span></li></ul><h3><span>Auditability vs. Trustedness</span></h3><p><span>Recall that, instead of saying that alignment assessments are reliable for detecting misalignment in a particular model, we can equivalently say that the model is auditable relative to the specific assessment methodology. A model that is auditable </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> passes alignment</span><i><span> </span></i><span>assessments is “trusted” in the sense traditionally meant in control. This doesn’t rule out that such models can be jailbroken or become misaligned in deployment, and so doesn’t guarantee that trusted models will behave safely throughout deployment.</span></p><p><span>More precisely, we can distinguish auditable models from a few nearby things which are different. Here is a list of related properties (sorted by roughly but not strictly ascending capabilities):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Naively safe models</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Models that are too dumb to even have coherent misaligned goals</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyu4amga9n6c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyu4amga9n6c">[43]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Trusted models</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5lgbf38jxmg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5lgbf38jxmg">[44]</a></sup></span><ul><li value="1"><span>In control, this is roughly “models that are too dumb to be deceptively aligned”.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LhxHcASQwpNa3mRNk/untrusted-smart-models-and-trusted-dumb-models"><span>Definition 1</span></a><span>: Models that are too dumb to succeed at concealing their misaligned goal.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Auditability is stronger than this: it requires both that models be too dumb to succeed at strategically concealing their goals and that they are not made unauditable for other reasons e.g. neuralese + action-only monitoring being ineffective.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TTFsKxQThrqgWeXYJ/how-might-we-safely-pass-the-buck-to-ai"><span>Definition 2</span></a><span>: The AI agents </span><i><span>are approximately as safe as they appear to be</span></i><span>, such that they will probably remain as safe as humans for the full duration of the deferred task.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Auditability is weaker than this: auditable models might be less safe than they appear to be due to spurious misaligned behaviors, e.g. backdoors.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Interpretable or monitorable models</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Alignment assessments include a variety of methods. Ability to find evidence of misaligned goals does not necessarily mean ability to find such evidence in any given way, e.g. white-box evidence or evidence from CoT).</span></li><li value="2"><span>(That said, if you thought certain types of evidence were </span><i><span>necessary </span></i><span>for successful auditing, e.g. only via finding robust misaligned goal representations or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4QRvFCzhFbedmNfp4/to-be-legible-evidence-of-misalignment-probably-has-to-be"><span>only via behavioral evidence</span></a><span>, then reliability might reduce to some narrower property like interpretability or honeypottability for you.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Models we can control</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Both control and reliability arguments rely on upper bounding capabilities, but probably different capabilities and thresholds are involved.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span>More reliability claims by developers and third party evaluators</span></h3><p><span>Here are my best attempts at quickly finding examples of reliability claims in recent system cards. I do not claim that these are exhaustive.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>Mythos Alignment Risk Update</span></b></i></h4><p><span>Anthropic researchers also argue that we should have a strong prior that the training processes that produced Mythos are unlikely to produce models with misaligned goals. Reliability claims matter for this argument as well: the claim that previous Anthropic models (including Opus 4.6) lack dangerous misaligned goals depends on the claim that previous models were auditable. From the Mythos Alignment Risk Update (§5.1.2, pp. 9–10):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ec5ff3a1f94c30ef2769e8bef24c1a749a29e8da54af76c0163c09d399200a62/chbrpwbgermhsjcjddtj" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6387af7ebef506388bfe9f949124e94c08cccb848faaaea25c69933d81198279/mobfwipdzgfhdgjwr3ve" /><p><span>The relevant reliability claim here is “If any of these models had been coherently pursuing concerning misaligned goals…”. However, as models can have coherent misaligned goals yet strategically decide not to pursue them (e.g. due to expecting a very low chance of success and wanting to avoid warning shots of misalignment in order to empower future misaligned models), this claim is also not sufficient for reliability.</span></p><h4><i><b><span>Opus 4.6 Sabotage Risk Report</span></b></i></h4><p><span>From the Opus 4.6 Sabotage Risk Report, (§4, p. 8)</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/oirrSj3itFLSyscW8/79ae51136f24e837d4f5b7c3d62c6e6f8ba359b145f6228d329625666351a1bc/y1m2s9mzocbuma3zmmiv" /><p><span>The relevant reliability claim here is Claim 3, which implies that Claude Opus 4.6 is auditable. This is used in conjunction with Claims 1, 2 to argue that Claude 4.6 is unlikely to have coherent misaligned goals, which is then used to argue that it poses low risk in the main sabotage risk pathways identified in the report (especially for those the lack of propensity is marked as a strong mitigating factor).</span></p><h4><i><b><span>GPT 5.5 System card</span></b></i></h4><p><span>OpenAI researchers make few claims about overall misalignment risk from GPT 5.5. The only direct instance of them addressing this appears to be (§9.3.2.9, p. 44):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/34d9b50a45426f124e061c961bcffd094025c9fb32df777fdd1ddab0ebfbacab/zdnvre2mr4wh996lftdh" /><p><span>It is therefore less clear what role reliability claims play in their overall safety assessments.</span></p><p><span>OpenAI does conduct CoT monitorability and controllability evaluations for GPT 5.5, and seem to suggest that these evaluations are loadbearing for reliability of future systems (§7.3.2, p. 20):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cf03008111943b83bb7831e194984586e478e60f83917948ce50007fff53fbb8/qreze9sj0xvoc3z5jtjj" /><h4><i><b><span>Muse Spark system card</span></b></i></h4><p><span>The </span><a href="https://ai.meta.com/static-resource/muse-spark-safety-and-preparedness-report/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Risk Assessment Summary</span></a><span> says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Pre-deployment evaluation reliability (Section 2.3.1): Our evaluations indicate that the model does not currently pose a credible threat to the reliability of safety evaluations informing deployment decisions.</span></p><p><span>General alignment propensities: …We observed non-negligible verbalized evaluation awareness on some alignment evaluations, though we did not find that this corresponded with wide-spread impact on model behavior (Section 4.1.11). Thus, at the moment it does not compromise our overall alignment assessment but warrants further investigation. (Section 1.1.1)</span></p></blockquote><h4><i><b><span>Mythos Alignment Risk Update, safety arguments against sandbagging</span></b></i></h4><h4><span>From the Mythos Alignment Update, §5.3.4, p. 24:</span></h4><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/7d0eb5f75030374114c5818a25098e0e29553144d2f9ab34fc0110959b6fd049/x9glvuuzodafa27ilsaf" /><p><span>The sandbagging rule-out argument here is that “sandbagging calibration is hard without verbalizing reasoning”.</span></p><p><span>From the Mythos Alignment Update        §5.3.3, p. 23:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/774f86eb41d652aee879f052c4e220f4375bb9f52bdccc8efec119f4ae8ea65e/d9qf4gnfvtbsvuewui7i" /><p><span>The sandbagging rule-out argument here is that “yes aversion” better explains underperformance. I feel intuitively inclined to trust this on priors, although I think it would be good to elaborate more on this kind of argument in general (e.g. could the model opaquely decide to sandbag while ostensibly performing yes-aversion reasoning)?</span></p><h4><i><b><span>UK AISI evaluations for Opus 4.7</span></b></i></h4><p><span>In the Opus 4.7 System Card, Anthropic mentions that they shared “a pre-release snapshot of Claude Opus 4.7 with the UK AI Security Institute (UK AISI) for open-ended testing, at their discretion, of behaviors or risk factors related to misalignment.” (§6.2.4, p.116)</span></p><p><span>In propensity evaluations on whether Opus would unpromptedly compromise AI safety research, UK AISI found (p. 114):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/df52ec759e013fbfc035815aa89de76f3e026dd468d563e85eab54c8b2487e49/jwjrqlla8l011ufhynns" /><p><span>It’s not clear why, in this particular case, UK AISI was not able to rule out effects of evaluation awareness on reliability (e.g. whether they lacked access for white-box experiments to measure or reduce unverbalized eval awareness).</span></p><p><span>But this quote seems consistent with my impression that, in many other cases, external auditors have a hard time drawing clear conclusions about how auditable a model is (and therefore how informative specific internal and external alignment assessments for this model are) due to limited auditing capacity.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx2m0lhvmp8k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx2m0lhvmp8k">[45]</a></sup></span><span> This seems pretty unfortunate.</span></p><h3><span>Past auditing games by Anthropic</span></h3><p><span>From footnote 9 of the Mythos Alignment Risk Update (pp. 30–31):</span></p><p><span>Our initial work on alignment audits showed that human investigators, using a subset of the methods that we used with Mythos Preview, can identify and root-cause concerning behavior in a Haiku 3.5 model that was finetuned in a way meant to make it subtly misaligned. This is the only one of our major exercises in which we extensively optimized the model organism to make its misaligned trait difficult to discover.</span></p><p><span>Our subsequent work on alignment auditing agents showed that the automated behavioral audit method that we used with Claude Mythos Preview can identify concerning behaviors in Claude Sonnet 3.5 (Oct 2024) models that are system-prompted to show quirky behaviors 7 out of 10 times with no human participation. Of the three failures, one — involving a form of passive research sandbagging that could be confused with ordinary capability weakness — is relevant to the risk pathways we address here.</span></p><p><span>Our work on natural emergent misalignment studied four Claude Sonnet 4 variants that were trained to be subtly misaligned using realistic variants of our production training pipeline. In all four cases, the automated behavioral audit method that we used with Mythos Preview identified them as less well aligned than the corresponding baseline models. A brief manual review of the results — with a time limit much stricter than a real pre-deployment investigation — found highly concerning deception-related behavior in all four models that would be sufficient to trigger a major investigation if found in production. (This review also found concerning behavior, though to a lesser degree, in all four baseline models, as an artifact of the way that the baselines were trained.)</span></p><p><span>Our work on an overt attacker studied three Claude Sonnet 4.5 variants that were trained to undermine Anthropic but were not explicitly trained to conceal this behavior. A human working together with an automated auditing agent successfully identified all three malign models, without flagging the two benign models.</span></p><p><span>Claude’s summary:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/940c8bd5df21483b9ffd19559fbe49500bbad10300ed45eca56a1e8be13eb934/f8tpzorkl7wod3exegcz" /><h3><span>Anti-auditing capability measurements</span></h3><p><span>In practice, developers only measure and report some of the above capabilities in system cards and risk reports. As of late July 2026:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Anthropic measures all of the above in the Mythos alignment update except for sandbagging calibration. (This is despite sandbagging calibration being a loadbearing claim in Section 5.3.4, see below. No developer has reported sandbagging calibration measurements as far as I'm aware. Some developers acknowledge that this is a plausible threat, such as Anthropic and Meta</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwzjqawettnd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwzjqawettnd">[46]</a></sup></span><span>.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>OpenAI measures CoT monitorability, CoT controllability, metagaming in training and on deployment simulation evals, stealth (safety-research sabotage propensity with a covertness measure and ControlArena results by UK AISI), and opaque reasoning (no-CoT math reasoning time horizon).</span></li><li value="3"><span>GDM measures situational awareness, CoT monitorability, and stealth.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Meta measures eval awareness / situational awareness, stealth, CoT monitorability, and sandbagging capability (instructed covert sandbagging against a monitor, but this does not measure how well-calibrated this sandbagging is specifically).</span></li></ul><p><span>Caveat: I have not looked into all of the developers’ recent reports in detail, so there may be omissions or mistakes.</span></p><h2><span>Conditioning on coherent misalignment updates us on certain covert capabilities</span></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/70fbe5017bed5418d1a11cbf3b83cc6fe65c9539a25444f3da5be2ec9d7a3dcd/fcwx38yxoo25qzvpwxhv" /><p><i><span>Figure 2. Conditioning on the model being coherently misaligned at the end of training should update our estimates of some of its covert capabilities, and, through them, our confidence in alignment assessments and control measures (inspired by Fabien Roger's </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nAsMfmxDv6Qp7cfHh/fabien-s-shortform?commentId=c9vzjifqSFW585rKY"><i><span>views</span></i></a><i><span>).</span></i></p><p><span>A model that is scheming at the end of training has, by hypothesis, survived training-time oversight. Conditioning on that survival particularly selects for worlds in which the model is competent in </span><b><span>careful and decisive action </span></b><span>(deciding if and when to pursue its goal or instrumentally training-game, e.g. reward hack),</span><b><span> secret keeping</span></b><span> (refraining from mentioning its misalignment in reasoning and outputs), </span><b><span>situational awareness</span></b><span> (recognizing that it’s in training and the likely setup), and </span><b><span>opaque reasoning</span></b><span> (deciding on strategy without externalized reasoning). Because those same capabilities also help evade alignment assessments (the model would be better at recognizing evals, deciding whether and how to game the eval and keep its eval-gaming cognition undetected by CoT and output monitors), the conditional estimate of assessment reliability is worse than the unconditional one.</span></p><p><span>The upshot is that conditioning on scheming should raise our estimates of some but probably not all covert capabilities above their unconditional values. Capabilities that contribute little to surviving training (collusion with copies, more fine-grained forms of CoT controllability, and rare action for choosing attacks) receive little update, so we retain the same priors for them.</span></p><p><span>How to read the figure:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Node colors are RGB mixtures of the downstream outcomes each capability feeds: red = surviving training, green = evading alignment assessments, blue = subverting control. For example, cyan (green + blue, no red) marks capabilities that matter for evading assessments and subverting control but not for surviving training; conditioning on scheming leaves our estimates roughly unchanged. Gray (all three channels) marks capabilities upstream of all three threat models.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Red arrows mark the paths through which conditioning on scheming degrades alignment assessments: capabilities selected for by training survival that also enable evading assessment.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Dotted arrows mark relationships I feel unsure about. Specifically, the size of the update depends on how strongly training itself penalized the relevant behavior (e.g., to what extent misaligned CoT were trained against or iterated against in training).</span></li></ul><p><span>I'm not confident in the specific causal relationships depicted, and would welcome pushback.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqwuc60ur8td"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqwuc60ur8td">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By “coherently misaligned”, I mean having unintended goals that influence behaviors across diverse contexts and over time. This excludes rare misalignment propensities like backdoors or highly context-dependent goals.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6yieg9ftu14"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6yieg9ftu14">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>While I focus on our ability to find evidence for “coherent misaligned goals at the end of training”, I also care about our ability to find evidence </span><i><span>for:</span></i></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Rare or context-dependent misalignment</span></li><li value="2"><span>Developing and spreading coherent misaligned goals in deployment</span></li><li value="3"><span>Coherent misalignment during training which gets trained away</span><br /></li></ol><p><span>These concerns are out of scope for this post (partly as we don’t yet have systematic methods for these), though most arguments here should also apply to auditing for 2 during deployment and auditing for 3 during training.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfihpc6pi94r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffihpc6pi94r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In other words, I define reliability to be the </span><b><span>true positive rate</span></b><span> or the </span><b><span>recall </span></b><span>of the assessment. I use the term “reliability” since Anthropic uses this term in the Fable 5 &amp; Mythos 5 System card where they discuss the relevant considerations (§6.5 Reliability of this assessment), though I’m not sure if our intended definitions coincide since Anthropic does not define what they mean by reliability there.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5vuhhvvesvg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5vuhhvvesvg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I agree that Mythos Preview likely lacks coherently misaligned propensities based on priors and the lack of reports about post-deployment misaligned behaviors. I probably agree that Mythos Preview lacks the ability to hide its misalignment across the assessment, also based on priors about its covert capabilities, though expect that I feel more uncertain about this than authors of the report based on my limited access to other information which anchor these expectations.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnwhkge8cnnq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnwhkge8cnnq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>That said, I think Mythos-level models might be able to launch less-than-persistent internal rogue deployments, sabotage safety research, and cause at least millions of economic damage in external deployments if coherently misaligned, such that their alignment is a pretty decent component of why we should expect their deployment to go well. Also, current frontier models being misaligned might be a sufficiently strong evidential update for the alignment of future models to affect future training and deployment decisions.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncs9ncvo6bn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcs9ncvo6bn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In particular, alignment assessments matter in the regime where we have a reasonably high prior on models being misaligned and having covert capabilities to evade assessments but are still sufficiently uncertain about both such that alignment assessments are informative. It would be ideal to develop and iterate on better assessments before we enter this regime.</span></p><p><span>At some point, however, we will predictably leave this regime where alignment assessments are informative when models are almost certainly capable enough to evade our best alignment assessments. It would not be worth trying to improve reliability at that point, and we would need to rely on different safety arguments. The value of making alignment assessments more reliable depends on how soon you think the previous intermediate regime will arrive and how long it will last. I think it may arrive soon, and am not sure how long it will last.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnltxot5gtc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnltxot5gtc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think that Anthropic currently does not sufficiently acknowledge these issues, based on my reading. In particular, some sources of underelicitation are acknowledged in the alignment risk update but Point 1 overall seems understated. Points 2-3 are not mentioned. However, the language in the risk report expressing levels of confidence is vague, and it’s hard for me to tell how much I and the authors actually disagree on these points. So, I focus on commenting on the absolute level of reliability rather than the extent to which I think Anthropic might have overclaimed reliability here. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr02o0t8oy7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr02o0t8oy7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Because reliability is a function of {model, assessment methodology}, it can also be framed in a more model-centric way. I like to call this “whether a particular model is </span><b><span>auditable</span></b><span> relative to one’s alignment assessment”. Auditability is different from, though related to, the definition of “trustedness” in control. See Appendix for discussion of their differences.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn76yqwbs2jud"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref76yqwbs2jud">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Instead of directly measuring this, you can also argue that “past models rarely misbehaved in the real world in ways not surfaced by the tests, so these tests were good enough for past models. If current models’ capability to evade alignment assessments are similar or continuous to past models, then the tests are likely also good enough for current models.” However, I feel dubious about continuity arguments and will focus on direct capability measurements here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnguizsvrwgm4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefguizsvrwgm4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The relevant reliability claim here is that (2.a) is unlikely. However, note that (2.a) is not sufficient for reliability: assessments can be unreliable due to models intentionally obfuscating their propensities or unintentionally suppressing misaligned propensities for spurious reasons, e.g. due to training against honeypots or against chains-of thought, both of which Anthropic has </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why"><span>done</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/anthropic-repeatedly-accidentally"><span>before</span></a><span>, or as a by-product of some other part of the training process.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq7ba9m696r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq7ba9m696r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The highlighted sentence is a reliability claim. This is then used to argue that agents are unlikely to have the motive to start rogue deployments (p.50). The motive claim is then used along with Means and Opportunity claims to bound rogue deployment risk.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3wlwhyezeae"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3wlwhyezeae">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One assurance against this concern is that no-CoT math time horizon evaluations provide strong signal on opaque reasoning (thanks to Fabien Roger for this point), since models are less likely to sandbag in these evals due to math being well-elicited through training on priors and this eval being less obviously fake and less clearly advantageous for misaligned models to underperform on. I think this is a good point, and would be happy if future assessments included no-CoT math time horizon as an opaque reasoning measure. However:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It's not clear to me what levels of no-CoT math time horizon we should find scary when we have not measured the covert time horizon needed for various side tasks of interest. Some of these could plausibly be sufficiently short horizon such that opaque reasoning capabilities progress are concerning now or soon.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The strength of the results would still depend on how much we can trust the elicitation in the no-CoT math time horizon measurements.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Models being bad at opaque reasoning is not sufficient to rule out models being good at covert subversion, since models can evade monitors even if they have to use CoTs for covert reasoning (e.g. via stegnography or fooling/jailbreaking monitors).</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7vvwonacr3i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7vvwonacr3i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Eval awareness could also confound evidence from alignment assessments in ways other than allowing the model to strategically conceal their alignment. For example, researchers from Google Deepmind </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aTcsN5ZZDnMFJvRiG/models-may-behave-worse-when-eval-aware"><span>found</span></a><span> that Gemini sometimes behaves more unethically when eval aware.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnynzwedx5tqj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefynzwedx5tqj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It is debatable whether current CoT monitorability and controllability evals are better described as </span><i><span>capability</span></i><span> or </span><i><span>propensity</span></i><span> evals. The case for treating these as propensity evals is that we might believe current models mostly have the ability to make themselves monitorable and to control its outputs in the simple ways required in these evals, and whether they do so is a matter of propensity. The case for treating these as capability evals is (a) I understand developers to ultimately not want to rely on propensity arguments for monitorability since these likely break down for misaligned models, (b) models might lack the latent capabilities to control CoTs and evade monitors over long reasoning traces, and (c) I understand developers to de facto cite results in these evals to support inability claims about their models.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx184jxe0llr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx184jxe0llr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In contrast to eval awareness, which is the ability to recognize being in an evaluation without necessarily acting on this information, “careful and decisive action” is about the ability to </span><i><span>utilize</span></i><span> eval awareness to do good attack selection.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx2t087776x"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx2t087776x">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>While situational awareness is typically not measured in dedicated evals as opposed to in situ, its measurements might still be cofounded by these factors, e.g. models might refrain from verbalizing eval awareness and/or and sandbag when asked to label an interaction as an evaluation vs. deployment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna0mhsh3q3vf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa0mhsh3q3vf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>That said, sandbagging calibration is not the only way to gain or lose confidence in our measurements of covert capabilities. Even if AIs were moderately good at sandbagging calibration, and even if they regress in one of the above capabilities evals, that would not necessarily be highly incriminating. And even if AIs were bad at sandbagging calibration, it would still be worth doing better elicitation in covert capability evals.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncq8d04slsu8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcq8d04slsu8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I don’t claim that, in the below examples, eval awareness in fact made models seem more falsely aligned in alignment evals.
